## STEGANOGRAPHY:
<note>original: "STEGANOGRAPHIA" — derived from the Greek words for "hidden" and "writing."</note>
-> *That is:* <-

# THE ART OF 
# REVEALING THE WILL 
# OF ONE’S MIND TO 

-> THOSE WHO ARE ABSENT <-
-> through hidden writing; <-
-> BY THE AUTHOR <-
-> **THE MOST REVEREND AND DISTINGUISHED MAN,** <-
-> JOHANNES TRITHEMIO, *Abbot of Sponheim,* and <-
-> *a most perfect Master* <insert>C<sup>u</sup> No 447.</insert> <-
-> *of Natural Magic.* <-
<note>Natural Magic in this context refers to the study of the hidden powers of nature and mathematics—including cryptography—rather than "black magic."</note>
-> PREFIXED TO THIS WORK IS ITS OWN KEY, OR <-
-> a true introduction composed by the Author himself; <-
-> **HITHERTO GREATLY DESIRED BY MANY,** <-
-> *but seen by very few:* <-
-> **But now made public for the benefit of students** <-
-> **of the more secret Philosophy.** <-
-> *With the Privilege and consent of the Superiors.* <-

--- Page 3 ---
<meta>This page follows the title page of the 1621 edition. It provides technical instructions to the reader regarding the color-coding system—red and black—used throughout the book's cryptographic tables and headings.</meta>

### TO THE READER.

You must be advised, Fair Reader, that regarding the letters **R.** and **S.** (which in this Work are at times expressed in numbers, characters, or words, either written above the text or added in the margin), the former is a mark of rubric <note>original: "rubricæ" — refers to red ink, traditionally used for headings and important instructions in manuscripts and early books.</note>, or the color red, while the latter is a mark of ink, or the color black. Furthermore, you shall observe this: that the individual titles of each chapter are likewise to be depicted and decorated in red. You may seek the significance of this matter from the Key <note>original: "Claue" — Referring to the *Clavis Steganographiae*, the author's guide to deciphering the work.</note> prefixed to the Work. Farewell.

<summary>A brief instructional note explaining the typographic conventions of the book, specifically that the letter 'R' denotes red text and 'S' denotes black ink, which is essential for understanding the cryptographic keys.</summary>
<keywords>rubric, red ink, black ink, Clavis, cryptographic instructions, typography, Trithemius</keywords>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page marks the formal beginning of the treatise following the instructional preface. It introduces the work's dedicatee, Philip, Count Palatine of the Rhine, and establishes the author's philosophical justification for using cryptography: the tradition of "occulting" sacred or dangerous knowledge from the unworthy, citing Moses, St. Jerome, and the ancient Greeks.</meta>

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut headpiece featuring symmetrical scrollwork, acanthus leaves, and stylized foliate motifs.</image-desc>

# HERE BEGINS THE BOOK
## THE FIRST BOOK OF THE STEGANOGRAPHY <note>original: "Steganographia" — A term coined by Trithemius from the Greek *steganos* (hidden) and *graphein* (to write); it refers to the art of hidden writing.</note>
### OF JOHN TRITHEMIUS, AB-
-> bot of Sponheim, of the Order of Saint Benedict, <-
-> in the Diocese of Mainz, <-

-> TO THE <-

## MOST SERENE PRINCE, LORD
### PHILIP, COUNT PALATINE
-> of the Rhine, Duke of Bavaria, and Prince- <-
-> Elector of the Holy Empire, etc. <-

<image-desc>A large historiated woodcut initial 'A' depicting a figure amidst vine-work and foliage.</image-desc>

*It is the opinion of the most learned men that the **most ancient Sages**, whom in the Greek tongue we call Philosophers, if they discovered any secrets <note>original: "arcana" — hidden or secret things.</note> of either nature or art, hid them by various means and figures so that they would not fall into the knowledge of wicked men. The more learned of the Jews also confirm that **Moses**, that most famous Leader of the Israelite people, in his description of the creation of Heaven and Earth, covered the ineffable secrets of mysteries with simple words. Even the Sainted and, among our own people, the most learned **Jerome**, affirms that nearly as many mysteries lie hidden in the Apocalypse of John <note>The biblical Book of Revelation.</note> as there are words. I pass over those Sages of the Greeks who were of no small reputation among their own; and I leave aside our own most learned **Philosophers and Poets**, who, devoting their labor to the writing of fables, delivered one thing to the unskilled and another to the learned by a sagacious invention <note>original: "sagaci adinuentione" — refers to the clever literary or symbolic devices used to hide deeper meanings.</note> in the sequence of a single narrative. These most studious lovers of Wisdom, even if I cannot perfectly imitate them due to the slowness of my intellect, I nevertheless do not fail to admire and read with what diligence I can. Because when those men, by their own study, [exceeded] other men in great [matters]...* <note>The sentence is interrupted here by the end of the page.</note>

<summary>This page serves as the formal dedication and introduction to the First Book of Steganography, where Trithemius argues that hiding knowledge through allegory and symbols is an ancient, respectable tradition used by Moses, St. Jerome, and the Sages.</summary>
<keywords>Steganography, John Trithemius, Philip Count Palatine, Moses, St. Jerome, secret knowledge, allegory, cryptography, Sponheim</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>Continuity: The previous page introduced the concept of ancient sages hiding knowledge. This page moves into the author's personal discovery and his dedication to Philip of Bavaria, while explaining why the book itself must be "encrypted" or hidden through a tradition he compares to the Kabbalah.</meta>

# PREFACE

*...I consider that I have thought [these things] out, striving against myself with a certain intensity so that I might grow inspired, I impel myself toward some kind of imitation of those who went before, even with the newest efforts. Nor, as I think, has my opinion entirely deceived me; for I have learned many things through continuous study of reading which I previously did not know: and I have opened an entrance for my thoughts to understand more secret things and matters altogether hidden from others.*

*For although I am not of such great erudition or industry that I would dare to profess that I have grasped in every part that way of hiding the mysteries of the ancient sages: nevertheless I have found certain modes—many and various—not entirely (as it seems to me) to be despised, by which I can communicate the most secret intention of my mind to another who knows this art, as widely as I wish, securely and without deception, suspicion, or the reproach of anyone, through open messengers or letters.*

*And I have committed to writing the new invention of this most secret art at the instance of the Most Serene Prince, Lord **Philip**, Count Palatine of the Rhine, Duke of Bavaria, and ever-invincible Prince Elector of the Holy Roman Empire, the wisest **Patron** <note>original: "Mæcenatis" — referring to Gaius Maecenas, the famous Roman protector of poets; here used to honor Philip as a great supporter of scholarship.</note> of all Philosophers, to whom no one seemed more worthy to me that this great secret should be revealed; and not without the greatest labors have I gathered it into the present volume.*

*Lest, however, this great secret reach the ears of common, ignorant, or wicked men, I have considered it no small part of my duty—since it teaches how to understand mysteries while the ignorant remain unaware—to wrap it in mysteries, so that no one from the number of the ignorant shall be hereafter, no one unless he is most studious, who could perfectly and fully penetrate the secret of this science according to our intention by his own virtue alone, unless by receiving it from a teacher, which the Hebrews call **Kabbalah** <note>original: "Cabalam" — Trithemius uses this term to describe a tradition of oral transmission where a teacher explains the "keys" to hidden knowledge to a worthy student.</note>, which is set over the most hidden mysteries.*

*Nor would the knowledge of this most secret art, if divulged to the wicked and the reprobate, harm the **Commonwealth** <note>original: "Reipub." (Republicae) — the state or public good.</note> less than it would benefit the good. Since indeed, just as good men and those studious of virtues use all inventions for good and common utility: so the evil and reprobate hunt for opportunities for themselves not only from bad things, but even from good and most holy institutions, by which they may become worse. A similar thing can happen to this most secret and otherwise useful and most honorable invention of ours; so that just as it offers itself as an instrument for the best things to good and holy men, so through...*

<meta>catchword: ...turned</meta>

<summary>Trithemius announces his discovery of a new method for secret communication and dedicates the work to Prince Philip of Bavaria. He explains that he has intentionally obscured the book's contents to protect the knowledge from the "wicked," asserting that it requires oral instruction (Kabbalah) to be fully understood.</summary>

<keywords>steganography, cryptography, Philip Count Palatine, Kabbalah, secrecy, intellectual protection, patronage, occult philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the author's warning regarding the dual nature of his secret art. He argues that while it is a tool for the virtuous, it could be dangerous if it falls into the hands of the wicked, specifically highlighting how it could be used to bypass linguistic barriers and social surveillance.</meta>

-># PREFACE<-

...to the perverse and impious, and provide a service for wicked deeds. For just as a good man and a lover of honesty may—for private or common good—safely, secretly, and without any suspicion from any mortal, notify another person who knows this art of the secret of his will whenever and as often as he wishes, perfectly, copiously, and in its entirety through letters that are open to everyone, whether plain or folded (so that no one, however learned or curious, can suspect anything of the sender’s secret; nor, even if they do suspect, can they detect it), and can intimate and express it at any time and at any distance; so too could any perverse, deceitful, or malicious person. Even one previously entirely ignorant of the Latin language, as soon as he has acquired this art (which, with me teaching, he could master in two days at most), could thereafter write letters in fitting Latin composition, in any narrative style, open, beautiful, and sufficiently ornate, to me or to another skilled in this art. In these, he would shroud the secret of his will—accessible to me alone—in such great mysteries by <term>Cabalistic</term> <note>original: "Cabalistica" — while today associated with Jewish mysticism, the author here refers to a method of encoding information through letter substitutions or symbolic "veiling."</note> power that it could be penetrated by no one at all, no matter how studious or learned, without the spirits of this art of which I am about to speak.

Thereafter, the learned and unlearned, man and woman, boy and old man, good and evil, modest and lustful, could write Latin, Greek, or "barbarian" <note>In this context, "barbarian" refers to any language other than Latin or Greek, such as German or French.</note> letters through all the climates of the world, free from all suspicion. Through these letters, they would show one thing openly to those who do not know the art, and another thing in secret to those who do. Not even the safe trust between married couples, contracted by sacrament, would remain if this science were made public to the reprobate: for a wife, though previously ignorant of the Latin language, could become sufficiently learned through modest, honest, and most holy words in any language or idiom to understand the evil and immodest mind and intention of an adulterous lover or fornicator—even with her husband delivering the letters and praising them as excellent. She could then send back her own desire in the same way, as broadly and copiously as she wished, most securely in the same or other letters with a beautiful and quite ornate sequence. 

Indeed, although this science is in itself excellent and sufficiently useful to the <term>Republic</term> <note>original: "Reipubl." — the "Respublica" or the State; the general public order.</note>, nevertheless, if it should come to the knowledge of the wicked (which God forbid), the order of the entire <term>Republic</term> could, in the course of time, be greatly disturbed; public faith could be endangered, along with all letters, legal instruments, records, and finally [the affairs] of men...

<meta>catchword: men</meta>

<summary>The author explains that his cryptographic art allows anyone—regardless of their prior education or gender—to send hidden messages disguised as innocent Latin letters, but warns that this power could destabilize society and marriage if misused.</summary>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s justification for his secret art, shifting from the potential for misuse to the infinite depth of the science itself. He begins to address the controversy of his methods, specifically the use of spirit names, to defend himself against accusations of black magic or demonic pacts.</meta>

-># PREFACE<-

*...human speech would be turned into the gravest suspicion: no one, no matter how holy and honest their writings, would believe without fear, and few would place trust in letters. Because, however honest and modest the words might be, guile, fraud, and deception would always be thought to lie beneath them; and men would become fearful of everything, being for the most part as suspicious of friends as of adversaries. Nor could anyone, even if they lived for a thousand years, become so learned and expert in this science with their teacher that there would not remain infinite ways—secretly, most occultly, and most securely—of writing in this very art, and, according to the will of any expert, ways sufficient for all operations, which he himself has not yet grasped with his preceptor. For just as the aerial spirits, both good and evil, created by the highest God for our ministry and progress (through whose intelligence all the secrets of this art are revealed), are to us without number, infinite and entirely incomprehensible: so too all the modes, ways, differences, qualities, and operations of this art of ours—which we call <term>Steganography</term> <note>original: "Steganographiam" — a term coined by the author, literally meaning "hidden writing."</note> (containing secrets, mysteries, and arcana perfectly open to no mortal, however studious or learned)—can never, in all eternity, be fully penetrated. For this science is a chaos of infinite depth, which no one can perfectly comprehend: because no matter how learned and expert you are in this art, you have always grasped less than what remains unknown to you. For this profound and most secret art has this property: it easily renders the student incomparably more learned, so to speak, than the master; provided that he is naturally disposed to proceed and is studious in those things which he has perceived in the <term>Cabalistic</term> <note>original: "Cabalistica" — here referring to a method of mystical or coded transmission rather than specifically Jewish Kabbalah.</note> tradition. And lest any future reader of this work—when in the process he frequently encounters the names, offices, orders, differences, properties, prayers, and any other operations of <term>spirits</term> <note>original: "spirituum" — in Trithemius's system, these spirits often represent the cryptographic keys or planetary influences used to transmit messages.</note> through whose intelligences all these secrets of this science are closed and opened—should believe or think me to be a <term>Necromancer</term> <note>original: "Necromanticum" — a sorcerer who conjures the dead, a charge the author vehemently denies.</note> and a <term>Magician</term> <note>original: "Magum"</note>, or to have contracted a pact with demons, or to have used or be using any other superstition: I have deemed it necessary and opportune to preserve my reputation and name from such great ruin, injury, fault, and stain by a solemn protestation in this <term>prologue</term> <note>original: "prologo"</note>, claiming it with the truth. I say, therefore, and before Almighty God, from whom nothing at all can be hidden,*

<summary>Trithemius describes Steganography as an inexhaustible, infinite science mediated by "spirits" and issues a formal defense against anticipated charges of black magic and necromancy.</summary>
<keywords>Steganography, hidden writing, spirits, Cabalistic tradition, necromancy, magic, cryptography, intellectual defense</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s formal defense of his work. While the previous page described the infinite depth and potentially frightening nature of Steganography, this page serves as a solemn, religious oath to reassure the reader (and the Church) that his methods are entirely holy and free from demonic influence.</meta>

-># PREFACE<-

<margin>The Author's protestation or oath regarding the integrity of this art, etc.</margin>
...can be done, and before Jesus Christ, His only-begotten Son, who is to judge the living and the dead <note>A standard liturgical phrase from the Nicene Creed, used here to emphasize the solemnity of the author's oath.</note>, I say in truth, I swear and protest: Each and every thing that I have said in this work, or am about to say, and all the properties, modes, figures, operations, traditions, receptions, formations, inventions, institutions, changes, alterations, and everything that pertains to its contemplation, invention, attainment, operation, and practice, either in part or in whole, and all things contained in this volume of ours, rest upon true Catholic and natural principles; and each and every thing is done with God, with a good conscience without injury to the Christian faith, with the integrity of Ecclesiastical tradition, without any superstition whatsoever, without idolatry, without any pact with evil spirits, whether explicit or implicit; without <term>fumigation</term> <note>original: "suffumigatione" — the ritual burning of incense or herbs often used in medieval magic to summon spirits.</note>, adoration, veneration, worship, sacrifice, or offering to demons, and without any fault or sin, whether venial or mortal: and each and every thing is done with truth, uprightness, sincerity, and purity. Thus, the science and practice of this invention may serve a good use and not be unbecoming to a wise man, a good and faithful Christian. For I too am a Christian, and voluntarily devoted to the monastic life: and I desire to live and conduct myself no otherwise than as becomes a true Christian and a monk professed under the <term>Rule of the Holy Father Benedict</term> <note>The author, Johannes Trithemius, was a Benedictine abbot; the Rule of St. Benedict is the foundational document for Western monasticism.</note>: and I have received the Catholic faith itself according to the tradition of the Holy Roman Church from my cradle, having been baptized in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, which I hold and believe with the universal Church of Christians, and as long as I live, with God's help, always keeping it firmly inviolate, I will hold it in heart, word, and deed, nor do I ever intend to deviate from it on any occasion. Far be it from me, therefore, to either learn or teach anything that is contrary to the Christian faith and purity, harmful to holy morals, or in any way adverse to my monastic purpose. I fear God, and I have sworn myself to His worship, from whom I shall not be separated either in life or (as I trust) in death.

<meta>catchword: This</meta>

<summary>In this passage, Johannes Trithemius issues a formal religious oath (protestatio) swearing that his work on Steganography is based on natural principles and is entirely free from black magic, demonic pacts, or superstition. He reaffirms his lifelong commitment to the Catholic faith and the Benedictine Order to shield himself from potential charges of heresy.</summary>

<keywords>Johannes Trithemius, oath of integrity, Steganography, Catholic faith, Benedictine Rule, anti-demonic, natural magic, monastic life</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s formal preface. On the previous page, Johannes Trithemius concluded a solemn religious oath (his "protestation") swearing that his work on Steganography is entirely natural and free from demonic magic. This page explains his reasons for including such an oath: to protect himself against accusations of heresy and to warn the reader that only those with patience and "sound minds" should attempt to learn these secrets.</meta>

-># PREFACE<-

I have placed this <term>protestation</term> <note>original: "protestatio" — here meaning a formal, public declaration or a solemn oath to clear oneself of suspicion.</note> of mine at the beginning of this entire work of ours for a reason, because I know there will be many who, when they cannot understand what we have written, will turn to insults and attribute our good and holy studies to evil arts or superstitious inventions. To these future critics, and to all who are about to read these <term>synthemata</term> <note>original: "synthemata" — a Greek-derived term referring to the symbolic arrangements, signs, or "compositions" that make up the author's secret system.</note> of ours, we earnestly pray that if they understand this secret tradition of ours, they keep it hidden forever and do not pour out such wonderful mysteries into the public. 

If, however, they do not understand (which we know will happen to many), let them learn before they criticize. Indeed, he shows himself to be a reckless judge who delivers a sentence before he has acknowledged the truth of the case. Learn this art first, and judge afterward; if you are unable to understand it, do not condemn the art itself (for it is good), but rather your own dull wits. 

For I know and am certain that no one of sound mind can criticize this work of ours, unless they happen to be completely ignorant of it. However, as for those who find it more familiar to despise wisdom than to learn it, I neither hope nor wish for them to penetrate these mysteries of ours.

<meta>catchword: CHAPTER</meta>

<summary>Trithemius justifies his opening oath as a defense against charges of sorcery and warns that the "dull-witted" are likely to mistake his secret art for superstition. He implores those who do understand his methods to maintain strict secrecy and avoid revealing these mysteries to the general public.</summary>

<keywords>protestation, secrecy, heresy, synthemata, wisdom vs ignorance, secret tradition, intellectual defense</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page begins the first chapter of the first book of the *Steganographia*. It follows the author's preface where he defended his work against charges of black magic. Here, Trithemius introduces the first class of "spirits" or cryptographic modes, beginning with a stern warning about their "rebellious" nature—likely a metaphor for the complexity and unreliability of the specific cipher system described in this section.</meta>

<image-desc>An ornate rectangular woodcut header featuring symmetrical foliate scrollwork and botanical motifs.</image-desc>

## Chapter I.

->**THE KEY AND OPERATION OF WHICH ARE HELD**<-
->*by the principal spirit Pamerfiel, Anoyr, and Madriel, through the ministry of Ebra, Sothean, Abrulges, and Itrasbiel. Also Nadres, Ormenu, Isules, Rablion, and Hamorphiel. To these is made the commission of all things with an exorcism.*<-

The operation of this first chapter is very difficult and full of dangers, due to the pride and rebellion of its spirits, who do not obey anyone unless he is most expert in this art. For they not only refuse to obey novices and those less proven in the art, but if they are pressed too hard, they frequently harm them and attack them with various illusions. They are malicious and unfaithful beyond all other <term>aerial spirits</term> <note>original: "aëreos spiritus" — in the context of this book, these are likely metaphors for specific types of fast-moving ciphers or communication methods associated with the air.</note>, and they obey no one at all unless compelled by the greatest <term>sacraments</term> <note>original: "sacramentis" — solemn oaths or sacred words used to "bind" the cipher or spirit into service.</note>; and the secret committed to them they often unfaithfully reveal to others. For as soon as they have been sent out with letters, they fly away; and breaking in without order to the person they are sent to—like a crowd fleeing from battle without a leader—they hurry furiously, and filling the air with their shouting, they often reveal the secrets to everyone around the sender. 

We advise, therefore, that no one intending to work in this art should compel them, nor anxiously seek their services. For they are stubborn and unfaithful, whereas you can find many among those we shall mention hereafter who are quite well-disposed, offering themselves willingly and ready for obedience. If, however, anyone wishes to experience their stubbornness for himself and prove that what we have said is true, let him know that this method must be observed: let him prepare the paper on which he is about to write with an invocation of the divine name, "In the name of the Father,

<meta>Catchword: and of the Son</meta>

<summary>Trithemius introduces the first category of secret communication (personified as the spirit Pamerfiel) but warns that it is exceptionally difficult and risky for beginners to use. He describes these "spirits" as rebellious and prone to revealing the very secrets they are meant to carry unless the practitioner is highly skilled.</summary>

<keywords>Pamerfiel, aerial spirits, secrecy, exorcism, cryptographic warnings, Madriel, spiritual rebellion</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructions for the first chapter, picking up from the description of spirits "filling the air" with chaos if not properly controlled.</meta>

# STEGANOGRAPHY

...and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit. Then, however, let him write in it <note>Referring to the parchment or letter.</note> whatever narrative he wishes, simple and open, which all readers may understand, in Latin, in his mother tongue, or in any other language. When he has sat down to write facing the East, let him compel the spirit thus:

*Pamersiel oshurmy delmuson Thafloyn peano charustca melany, lyaminto colchan, paroys, madyn, moeilay, bultre* <margin>†</margin> *atloor don melcoue peloin, ibutsyl meon mysbreath alinidriaco person. Crisolnay, lemon afosle mydar, icoriel pean thalmo, afophiel il notreon banyel ocrimos esteuor naelma befrona thulaomor fronian beldodrayn bon otalmefgo mero fas elnathyn bosramoth.* <note>While these appear to be "barbarous names" or magical incantations, in the context of Trithemius's work, these strings of words often function as the cryptographic keys or the hidden message itself, disguised as an occult ritual.</note>

When he has said these things, and it seems to him that the spirits are present to obey, let him continue the work begun. But if they have not yet appeared, let him repeat what was said as many times as necessary until they appear; or let him cease entirely, lest they, being too greatly compelled, harm the operator. <note>Trithemius frequently uses the threat of "harm" by spirits as a metaphor for the errors and "corruption" of data that occur when a cipher is used incorrectly.</note> When you have completed the letters, send them with intent by a messenger to a friend proven in the art; when he has received them, let him say this <term>conjuration</term> <note>original: "coniurationem" — here, the decryption key or protocol required to read the hidden text.</note>:

*Lamarton anoyr bulon madriel traschion ebrasothea panthenon nabrulges Camery itrasbier rubanthy nadres Calmosi ormenulan, ytules demy rabion hamorphyn.*

Once these things are said, he will soon understand your mind perfectly, as the spirits now offer themselves of their own accord with force and shouting, so much so that often others present may also perceive the sender's secret. But observe that in all letters you send written by this art, you should fix a <term>proper sign</term> <note>original: "proprium signum" — likely a specific character or symbol indicating which cipher key (or "spirit") was used to encode the message.</note>, so that the one to whom you write may know through which spirits you have operated. For if he were to operate through some spirits to understand, and you through others to send, they would never obey him, but because of the improper operation, they would both harm him and fail to reveal your secret. For all the <term>spirits</term> <note>In Trithemius's system, "spirits" represent specific cryptographic methods or tables.</note> we use in this art guard only the orders and duties committed to them, and do not interfere with others at all. Consider diligently all that we have said in this chapter, and you will more easily be able to understand what we are going to say in the following ones.

<meta>catchword: And that</meta>

<summary>This page outlines the ritualistic procedure for encoding and decoding messages using the "spirit" Pamersiel, emphasizing the need for the sender and receiver to use matching "signs" or keys. Trithemius uses the language of demonic conjuration to mask what is essentially a complex manual for steganographic communication.</summary>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the practical application of the steganographic methods described in the previous chapter, moving from the theory of spirits to a concrete example of how a secret message is hidden within a mundane-looking letter.</meta>

BOOK TWO.
A 2
<meta>catchword: your</meta>

And so that we may prove what we have said by an example, let us set down our <term>intent</term> <note>original: "intentionem" — in this context, the actual secret message to be conveyed.</note> in the common tongue, which we will subsequently communicate to a friend in the manner described before.

<margin>Roth.</margin>
### LET THIS BE THE SECRET INTENTION OF THE MIND
->of some prince, which he wishes his<-
->absent vassal to know.<-

Dear faithful servant: we are minded, for honest cause, to wage war against those of Strasbourg and to fall upon them with all our might on the next Wednesday after the feast of Saints Vitus and Modestus. <note>The feast of Saints Vitus, Modestus, and Crescentia is June 15th. The "Wednesday after" would be the specific date for the military strike.</note> It is therefore our earnest desire of you, as you are bound to us by oath and vow and tied to faithful service: that you appear on that same day well-equipped, according to your ability, at four hours after midnight by Straussenburg in the forest (Here were the letters so placed) <note>original: "Hic ita erant literæ positæ" — This likely refers to the layout of the text in the original cryptographic grid.</note> where you would find further instructions to follow; do not fail to appear. We wish to acknowledge this to you in grace, and keep this our plan with you in high secrecy, etc.

### TO NOTIFY THIS SECRET
<margin>Roth.</margin>
->To the vassal, let it be written with a broader<-
->intent of the sender in this way or another:<-

> Almighty eternal God, who created all things from nothing, have mercy on us through the most bitter passion of your only-begotten Son dying on the cross, who, conceived of the Holy Spirit without male union, was born of the most pure ever-Virgin Mary, and delivering himself for us voluntarily unto death, by his most holy blood freed our souls from death. Hear us miserable ones, O most clement Father, and through the shedding of the most sacred blood of the same your most sweet Son our Lord Jesus Christ, forgive us our sins and infuse our hearts with your grace, so that loving you in all things and above all with sincere fervor, we may always obey your commands with devotion. And continually advancing in mutual love, let us henceforth love nothing of the earth, and seek nothing perishable. For you are the Lord our God, who created us in your image and likeness from the beginning when we were not: and through your beloved son...

...you redeemed <note>The previous page ended with "Son," so this refers to Jesus Christ.</note> when we were lost: whom you raised from the dead on the third day, after he had died for our sins and been buried; and whom you showed alive, with much consolation, to his rejoicing disciples for forty days. Through him we ask you, O most merciful Father, hear us and have mercy on us now and in that terrible hour in which we are to be judged through your son. Look upon us, most merciful God: for we are fragile and pitiable sinners, naked of good works, who have offended your most holy Majesty in many ways by sinning. But having turned to penance by your mercy, we groan and weep for our sins: humbly praying through Jesus Christ your son, that you turn your indignation away from us, and mercifully forgive our past crimes, leading us, once this present exile is ended, to the pleasantness of the heavenly homeland. Johannes Trithemius wrote this.

Write this kind of simple and open narrative—or any other you wish that everyone may read and understand and which contains no secret, and which you do not fear coming into anyone's sight—on the aforementioned paper. Do this with the <term>conjuration of Spirits</term> <note>Trithemius describes a ritual where the sender speaks to spirits to "charge" the letter with a hidden meaning.</note> mentioned before, with your face turned toward the East. Once it is written, express your secret <term>intent</term> <note>original: "intentionem" — the hidden message regarding the attack on Strasbourg.</note>—whatever you wish the absent person to know—aloud before the spirits, calling them to witness that they should act faithfully, and they will immediately fly away. When the one to whom they are sent receives your letter, having recognized the <term>sign of the East</term> <note>A subtle mark on the letter indicating it contains a hidden message requiring the ritual.</note>, let him turn his face toward the East and say the conjuration we provided before: *Lamarton, anoyr* <note>These are "words of power" or names of spirits used to unlock the message.</note>: and he will immediately understand your secret intent which you committed to the Spirits

# TO KNOW THE PLACES OF SPIRITS,
### *their names & figures, with the note placed below.*

BUT since it is necessary for every practitioner in this <term>science</term> <note>original: "scientia"; Trithemius uses this word to describe his system of secret communication, which he masks as a form of angel magic.</note> to know the locations and names of the principal Spirits and their signs—lest through ignorance one calls upon a spirit from the north while waiting in the south, which would not only hinder the intended purpose but could even harm the practitioner—I shall consequently set down for you, within a circle, the regions in which the principal Spirits dwell, along with their names and signs.

-> Red <- <note>original: "Rub."; indicates a rubric or text that would have been printed in red ink.</note>

I would have you understand "East" here not as the place where the Sun rises on any given day, but as that place in which it was created from the beginning, which we call the <term>equinoctial</term> <note>The celestial equator; Trithemius is specifying that his directions are based on fixed cosmic coordinates rather than the local, shifting sunrise.</note>. ### CHAPTER II.

<margin>Red <note>original: "Roth."; referring to the spirits categorized under red ink in the master table.</note></margin>
### THE PRINCE OF WHICH IS CALLED PADIEL
->*The East Wind, having under him 10,000 day ministers, 200,000*<-
->*night ministers, and many other sub-servants, whose*<-
->*number is uncertain.*<-

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'C' inside a square frame featuring a landscape with a building and a winding path.</image-desc>
Since, as we said in the previous Chapter, Pamerfiel—along with his aforementioned Spirits—is relentless and unfaithful, and does not obey everyone indiscriminately who desires to work in this art, but only the most expert in it; we wish in this present Chapter to make the art safer. We intend to demonstrate a way of working in it through better Spirits who are benevolent, prompt, and cheerful in obeying the one calling, who—when called in the proper manner and time—come without delay, and faithfully carry out what has been committed to them without any deception. The first principal emperor and president of this operation is called Padiel, by the name <term>Subsolanus</term> <note>original: "Subsolanus"; the Latin name for the East Wind, literally "under the sun."</note>, from the circular mansion; which is the first mansion after the east. He has under him ten thousand who preside over the day and all the daytime operations of this art, who command and bring forth the nocturnal Spirits hiding in the darkness, who number two hundred thousand; they all flee the light

...and day, except for one who acts as a messenger to the presiding Spirits: they are all good and benevolent, and they do not harm the practitioner unless he is malicious or unskilled in the art. It is not necessary to summon them all at once; a few are sufficient, and sometimes one is enough to satisfy the practitioner, whether the operation takes place by night or by day. For since they are benevolent, quiet, and tranquil, they are more often solitary than rowdy in a crowd. Therefore, when you wish to work through them and disclose the secret of your mind to someone—no matter how distant—through their agency, do as follows: Write on paper previously prepared in the proper manner, as you know, whatever narrative you wish and in whatever language; for it does not matter, because the recipient is not intended to perceive your mind from the paper, but from the Spirits. While writing, turn yourself toward the East <note>original: "subsolanum," referring to the direction of the East Wind</note>, and when finished, recite this conjuration:

<note>While presented as a magical incantation, these "spirit names" and "words of power" are actually a cover for a cryptographic system. In Trithemius's method, the actual message is hidden within these nonsense words through a specific letter-replacement code.</note>

When you have completed these words, you will see two, or at least one, of the Spirits calmly present and ready to obey your will. > Padiel ariel vanerhon chio tarson phymatto merphon ampriscoleda-
> barym, elsophroy mesarpon amcorsy, paneryn atle pachunigel 
> thearan vtrul vt solubito beslonty las gomadyn triamy mesar-
> nothy.

However, no one who is malicious or wicked will easily be able to work in this art; but the better and purer a person is, the more willingly the Spirits... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence; likely "will serve him."</note>

### THE SECRET OF THE INTENTION, WHICH IS committed not to letters, but to the Spirits of the second mansion.

<margin>Roth.</margin>
*Someone has been reported to a prince, accused of some crime of high treason <term>high treason (laesa maiestas): literally "injured majesty," a crime against the state or sovereign</term> or any other offense. The prince wants him punished by his official, from whom he is far distant, but he does not want to write those specific instructions to him, lest the letters be discovered for any reason and the accused become warned and flee, or resist by joining forces with friends, or attempt some sinister plot against the prince or the laws of the country. He writes this or any other narrative whatever to his prefect, which he does not fear to have read by everyone: but he commits the secret message to the Spirit, in the manner we have said before. The Spirit will faithfully narrate the secret to the person to whom it is sent, provided they were previously instructed in this art.*

*Sincere affection. I send you a prayer for the devotion of penitents, quite beautifully composed and brief, which the full benevolence of my mind did not wish to keep for myself alone, and it is as follows:*

> *Free us, Jesus Christ, eternal savior of all good things, and forgive the sins of the guilty; hear our groans, calming the storms of vices, and renewing the oldness of conscience, lead us back to the eternal paradise. Good Jesus, remit to us who pray our sins: since we have offended gravely and have often relapsed. Sweetest savior, heal our infirmities, we pray. Be kind to those who ask you, most pious Jesus, and hear us, your supplicating servants. We are men involved in constant miseries, and immersed in the greatest storms. Rescue us, most victorious consoler of the troubled; since our life, buried in constant unhappiness, is corrupted. Have mercy, most merciful Jesus savior, look upon the humility of those who love you and do not permit us, exiles, to be drowned in the mud and filth <note>The Latin "turpitudine" is faded here.</note> of vices, in the love of the world. Up to this point we lie <note>The Latin "iacuimus" is partially obscured.</note> torpid, and entangled in vices, our small worth—*

### CHAPTER III.

### WHOSE PRINCE IS CAMUEL, HAVING UNDER
<margin>Note.</margin>
*him ten spirits presiding over daytime operations and ten similarly over nighttime operations, with just as many sub-servants, whose duty it is to announce the sender's arrival, ways, and journey.*

<image-desc>A decorative drop cap 'T' showing a figure, possibly a monk or gardener, within a lush landscape or garden, framed by a square border of swirling leaves and vines.</image-desc>
The first and supreme Spirit of the third mansion <note>term: mansion (Latin: mansio); in this context, it refers to a specific "office" or celestial station in Trithemius’s system</note> toward the East <note>original: "Eurum," referring to the Southeast wind</note> is called **Camuel**. He is very benevolent and prompt to send his substitutes to the one who operates in this science according to the proper form. He has few ministers indeed, but all are good, voluntary, and faithful—namely ten who preside over the day and ten who preside over the night. These are always sent in pairs to the operator from both orders, since those who preside over the day do not flee the darkness, nor do those who preside over the night flee the light. Always, where necessity demands, in every commission of the operator, one is aided by the other; and when they appear visibly, then one is usually seen in a beautiful garment of mixed color, and the other in the form of bright splendor. Yet they often appear by sensible effect alone <note>meaning they can be felt or heard</note> but are not seen, unless it so pleases the operator. This **Camuel**, with his ministers and sub-servants, has authority and power to announce to an absent friend the status, will, condition, path, journeys, arrival, and departure of the sender, whenever the one who operates in this art desires. When, therefore, you wish to operate according to your will through the angels of this mansion, you must first know who the ten angels of Camuel are, who

Here in this table you have the ten angels of Camuel who preside over the day with the number of their substitutes, and just as many who preside over the night, also with their own servants. However, the last ones, as you see, have no substitute, because 0 <note>Trithemius uses the numeral 'o' for zero</note>, as you know, signifies nothing at all in number. Therefore, when you wish to work through these angels, prepare your paper as is the custom; and facing the East <note>original: "Eurum," the southeast wind</note> write any story you wish, and in whatever language you like, which does not need to be hidden: once it is finished, read the following conjuration <term>conjuration (Latin: coniuratio); in this system, the "prayer" or "spell" is actually a set of instructions for the recipient to identify which cipher key to use</term> silently.

> Camuel aperoys, melym meuomanial, casmoyn eralti busaco aeli lumar photytion theor besamys, aneal Cabelonyr thiamo vesonthy.
<note>This "conjuration" consists of "barbarous names"—words that sound like a magical language but function as the steganographic key.</note>

Having completed this conjuration in silence, give your commission to the spirits who are present, just as you know; and send them forth, performing everything in silence and away from noise if possible. Afterwards, give the letter to whichever messenger you wish to carry it—open, if you like—and let him go, for the Spirits will not neglect their duty, whether the messenger arrives quickly or slowly. Have no doubt about this, for they are faithful. It is necessary, however, that you know which Spirit you wish to send from those mentioned above, so that in the conjuration you express his name immediately after the principal spirit, and do this in all operations where the names of the angels to be sent are placed in the chapter itself. But now let us provide an example <note>original: "similitudinem," a likeness or demonstration</note> of what we have said concerning those things which belong to the office of Camuel and his subject Spirits.

BOOK SECOND.
B 3
<meta>catchword: LET</meta>

# LET THIS BE THE MOST SECRET INTENTION OF MY MIND. Se. Roth.
## which I wish the absent person to know through the
## aforementioned Spirits.

*I am situated at Rome, and I understand from the most secret council of the Supreme Pontiff <note>The Pope</note> that he wishes to transfer the Roman Empire itself to the Franks <note>The French</note>. I desire to advise the princes of Germany: I cannot do so by messengers, whom I do not trust; I cannot do so by letters, because in all the cities of Italy there are deputies stationed at the gates who search those passing through most exactly. I write, therefore, one devout prayer, which I send as an open document by a messenger, along with other familiar closed letters to the same princes. I compel the Spirits of the third mansion and I command them to follow the letters to be sent. And when they have been called by the recipient, let them not fail to reveal to him my secret intention, which I did not dare to commit to letters. They come, they go, they obey.*

*We all die, since we all have contracted the hereditary guilt of original sin: and no one lives who will not die in a very short time: we contracted original offense in Adam and Eve. Moreover, all men die the death of the body on account of the wretched rashness of Mother Eve. Indeed, Jesus, the Victor over death, has put to flight the death of the soul by liberating us. O Jesus Christ crucified, look upon our humility because we are miserable, unhappy, and vexed by many tribulations. Therefore, most merciful King of the heavens, we ask: grant us contrition and pardon of all sins on account of your extraordinary humility by which you redeemed us from the sorrowful darkness of hell and noxious passions. O our beatitude, most merciful Jesus, O sweetest lover of innocence, what shall I return to you? You, holy and innocent, suffer for us. Where shall we flee when you come to the examination of reason? You will come with power to render a reward to each one according to his conscience. And all things will be naked to you: nor will anything be hidden from you of which you do not have knowledge. Therefore, living brothers, do penance, and anticipate the strict examination of Almighty God with works of mercy.*

*Wipe away crimes with tears and, by relieving the labors of the poor, make God favorable to you. Since he is kind to those who call upon him. Therefore, while there is time, seek his mercy. Always give thanks to him who redeemed you, Jesus Christ crucified for the salvation of men who fear the Lord.*

<summary>Trithemius provides a hypothetical scenario where a spy in Rome must warn German princes of a Papal plot; he demonstrates how a secret message is hidden within a standard pious prayer about mortality and redemption.</summary>
<keywords>clandestine communication, Holy Roman Empire, Papacy, Camuel, Third Mansion, steganography, original sin, penance</keywords>

# STEGANOGRAPHY

<margin>Roth. <note>Likely a reference to the recipient or a specific sub-category of the system.</note></margin>

### LET THIS BE ANOTHER FORM OF SECRETS;
#### which is committed to the Spirits.

*A secret remains stored in my mind regarding my upcoming visit to a friend—be they male or female—concerning our next secret meeting. I do not dare to write this very thing down, lest it become public, for a grave reason forbids it; nor can I send it by a messenger, as the danger is the same. I call a spirit, I commit the matter to it; it comes, it goes, it delivers the secret most secretly. I am safe through the form of the letters I send; let the letter be as familiar and casual as you please, so that the opportunity for delivery is not lost.*

*Greetings. The bearer of the present letter, brother Arnoldus Bostius <note>Arnold Bostius (1445–1499) was a Carmelite monk and a fellow humanist scholar who corresponded frequently with Trithemius.</note>, has composed many admirable volumes on various subjects. As you will find written at the end of our book concerning Ecclesiastical writers <note>Trithemius’s own work, *De Scriptoribus Ecclesiasticis* (1494), a massive bibliography of Christian authors.</note>—which I sent to you some time ago—among many other things where mention is made of living authors, you will find him listed. I commend this great man, notable for his religious devotion and the beauty of his arts, to your sincerity, my most dear patron. I ask most earnestly that you receive him with that familiarity with which you are accustomed to welcome all those joined to us in friendship. He will reveal many secrets of nature to you, and will be able to delight you with the sweetest conversation, if you so desire. I pray that you take care to return to me as soon as possible my codex of magical experiments which you have. I will soon send you the volume of Synesius <note>Synesius of Cyrene, an ancient philosopher whose works were highly valued by Renaissance occultists.</note> by the power of the spirits once you have returned my experiments to me. Take care of yourself so that you may be well. Write back to me often. Given in Sponheim on the tenth of March <note>Original: "sexto Iduum Martij," the sixth day before the Ides of March.</note> in the year of the Lord’s incarnation 1500. Johannes Trithemius, Abbot.* — *The one who receives the letters, to whom you sent by means of the spirit, should do what he knows is to be done in this art: and having recognized the circular sign, let him turn toward the East <note>Original: *Eurum*, the East wind.</note> and in silence say this invocation:*

*Camuel Busarcha, menaton enatiel, meran sayr abasremon, naculi pesarum nadru lasmon enoti chamabet usear lesponty abrulmy pen sayr thubarym, gonayr asmon friacha rynon otry hamerson, buccurmy pedauellon.* <note>In the context of Trithemius’s system, these seemingly nonsensical magical words are actually a "steganographic" cipher. The secret message is hidden within these syllables, usually extracted by taking the first and last letters of words or following a specific pattern known to the recipient.</note>

*Once these things have been said, let him take the letters in hand again, looking toward the East as you know. It should be noted that all these spirits with their prince are located in the East, and therefore it is necessary that everyone who works through them turns that way: or if he cannot, he should at least place the letters facing that way when he has received them. Observe and understand what we have said to you.*

# BOOK ONE.

### CHAPTER IV.

<margin>Roth. <note>Likely a reference to a specific sub-classification or a recipient of the work, appearing consistently in the margins of these chapters.</note></margin>

### WHOSE PRINCE, ASELIEL, DWELLS AT THE South-southeast, having under him 40 principal substitutes with their servants; and they preside over those things which pertain to love.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'Q' inside a square border with foliate motifs.</image-desc>The **fourth mansion** from the East, in the South-southeast, is held by the great prince **Aseliel** with his spirits, of whom ten preside over operations in this <term>art<note>The "art" refers to Steganography, Trithemius's method of hiding secret messages within seemingly innocent text or through the invocation of spirits.</note></term> by day, and twenty by night. The power and office of all these is to report those things which pertain to the **love of women**. Moreover, these have thirty as principals under them, and just as many others whom they send in order to their duties, whenever they have been called by an operator in the proper manner. Again, under these there are others, of whom there is a great number. 

But when you wish to work through the prince of this mansion in <term>Steganography<note>Originally meaning "hidden writing," here it refers to the spiritual-telepathic method described by Trithemius.</note></term>, there is no need to call upon all the spirits subject to him; rather, one or two are sufficient for you, whether the operation be by night or by day. I will tell you, therefore, the names of a few, with the number of those subservient to them. From these you should always call one or two as you wish; you do not need more, unless the operation is for diverse purposes. This is the table:

Behold, you now have sixteen princes from among the greater ones who are under the great **Asiel** <note>original: "Asiel", a variant spelling of the prince Aseliel mentioned in the chapter heading.</note>. Eight of these preside over the day, and eight preside over the night, and each of them has 20 subject servants whom they send to their duties as often as it shall be necessary. All the princes are good, benevolent, and most ready to obey; but their servants are not all so, for they are sometimes **proud and implacable**, especially toward those whom they consider to be less perfected in the art. These indeed...

# STEGANOGRAPHY
[Page 78]

...fore, sixteen princes with their subjects are sufficient for you to announce all things, nor is there a need for you to know or call upon more. However, when you wish to work in Steganography through Aseliel, following the practices previously established as customary in this art, observe the location of his dwelling, which is in the **South-southeast**, and call upon whichever one of the spirits mentioned above you wish, according to his proper time. This is the conjuration <note>In the context of Trithemius’s work, these "conjurations" often consist of strings of words that serve as a cryptographic key or a signal to the recipient rather than a literal magical spell</note>: *Aseliel aproyfy, melym, thulnear casinoyn, mauear burson, charny demorphaon, Theoma asmeryn diuiel, casponti vearly basanys, ernoti chaua lorson.* Once the conjurations have been completed according to custom, add the other things which are customarily done in the art, and a most certain effect of your operation will follow. Let us set forth an example.

Imagine a secret of your mind. You have a mistress with whom you are in love, and she no less with you; but you are unable to approach or speak to her because of the guards watching her. You have finally devised a plan by which you might be able to win her, but it is necessary to advise her of it first. You cannot do it yourself; you dare not do it by letters lest they fall into the hands of others; you do not decide to commit the matter to messengers or old women <note>A common literary trope in the Renaissance, where "old women" or "crones" were often depicted as go-betweens or procurers in secret love affairs</note>, lest they later betray you. Therefore, you keep the matter hidden for a time, trusting that it cannot be made public. You call the invisible ministers of the art, you write a letter that is in no way suspicious, and you send it to your mistress even by way of the guards themselves; they praise the writing and offer it to the mistress to be read. She, recognizing the sign, hears the hidden message after the messenger has been called <note>The "invisible messenger" refers to the secret meaning decoded by the recipient</note>, hears the plan, and sends back her consent: you approach, you enter, and you enjoy her company.

> *The death of Jesus Christ gave life to the human race, whose life, though innocently afflicted, freed us from all calamity. Therefore, let us honor His humility within us by resisting the temptations of vice and by persisting in all good works. Jesus Christ saved our souls. Let us give eternal thanks to our most pious Redeemer, since He has led everyone back to safe spoils <note>original: "exuuias," referring to the spoils of war or the remains of the dead; here, it likely signifies the souls rescued from death</note>; let us praise His name with fervor at all times, coming before His most holy face with our prayers; let us live virtuously in the love of righteousness, casting away the tumult of worldly affairs, let us follow the rule of justice, and let us most devoutly resist harmful vices, and [wash away] our negligences with tears...* <note>The Latin text ends mid-sentence with the prefix "ab-", which is completed on the next page by the catchword "luamus" (let us wash away). This pious-sounding passage is a "mask" or cover text: to an outsider, it looks like a religious meditation, but it hides a secret message.</note>

# BOOK ONE.

...[let us] wash away <note>original: "luamus", likely the end of "abluamus" or "soluamus" (let us pay/atone)</note> [our sins] in the greatest anxiety, mindful of the future judgment, the inestimable horror of which is in no way inferior to the pains of hell. In the year of the Lord’s birth 1506, on the Ides of March <note>March 15th</note>. When he to whom they are sent, being skilled in this art, receives these or similar letters, let him do what he knows must be done according to the established rules; let him turn himself toward the direction of the **South-southeast** <note>original: "Euroaustri"</note>. This is its sign: <image-desc>An occult astrological or alchemical sigil consisting of a horizontal line with various loops, dots, and a character resembling a stylized '2' at the end.</image-desc> And let him read the summoning of spirits <note>In this system, these "summonings" are actually strings of words where specific letters or patterns form the hidden message</note>, which is as follows: *Aseliel murnea casmodyn bularcha vadusy naty belron diuiel arsephonti sipa normys orleuo cadon Veneti basramyn.* Once these have been summoned, let him continue the operation of the art, and he will hear the secret of the sender.

### CHAPTER V.

## WHOSE SUPREME PRINCE IS BARMIEL [RED] <note>original: "Roth", the German word for Red, used here as a category label in the cryptographic system</note>.

->by name, he dwells in the **South**, having under him 10 princes who preside over the day, and 20 who preside over the night with their servants: and they are for announcing secrets which pertain to the surrender of castles <term>traditiones castrorum: "Traditio" can mean "tradition," but in a military context, it refers to the handing over or surrender of a fortified position</term>.<-

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial letter V containing a scene with a person, likely a scholar or monk, seated at a desk and engaged in writing or study.</image-desc>The great prince of the **fifth mansion** in the South, toward the midday point, is called Barmiel. He has under his dominion 10 principal dukes who preside over the day with their sub-servants, and 20 in number who preside over the nocturnal operations with their attendants. They are more numerous than the day-spirits because the operation of this mansion occurs more frequently in the night than in the day. For their office is to report secret surrenders of castles and cities, especially during the night. When you wish to work in this art through Barmiel, call upon one of his princes, whichever you wish; he will come to you immediately with 20 servants, for none of the princes of this mansion are accustomed to travel alone or with fewer attendants. Entrust your secret not to the attendants, for they are proud and malicious, but to the prince, because he is good, calm, faithful, and very benevolent. But now I will name for you eight of the day-princes and similarly eight of the nocturnal ones, who are better and more prompt to obey, nor do we require more.

You have here 16 spirits, twelve of whom each have 20 ministers with whom they are accustomed to appear to the one calling. The remaining four, under whom a 0 is placed (which signifies nothing), have no subservient ministers, but always come alone; they are most faithful in their service and tireless in everything you command them. Therefore, wishing to operate through any of them, having first performed those things which are to be performed in this art <note>This refers to the preparatory rituals or cryptographic setups described earlier in the book</note>, you should say this conjuration toward the **South**: Barmiel buras melo charnotiel malapos veno masphian albryon, chasmia peluo morophon apluer charmya noty Mesron alraco caspiel hoalno chorben ouear aserea cralnoty carephon elcsor bumely nesitan ármiy tu faron. <note>In Trithemius's system, these long "conjurations" are actually the carrier for the hidden message; specific letters within these nonsense words map to the actual secret text</note> After you have completed the command, entrust the secret to the appearing spirit whom you have called; moreover, send the letters by whomever you wish, and in whatever manner you wish. But let us use an example.

---

<margin>Red</margin>
->**LET US SET DOWN THE KING’S SECRET, WHICH**<-
->*should not be entrusted to anyone in any way.*<-

*A certain king or prince is unable to obtain a city or fortification by arms as he desires; he attempts the matter by craft, corrupts the guards with a bribe, but they trust no one with the plan—not even letters—lest, if caught, the king does not achieve his intent and the guards lose their lives along with the bribe. The prince turns himself to the Mansion of Barmiel, calls the messenger spirit, and entrusts the hidden matter: the spirit comes, delivers it to the messenger, it is seized at night, and no one knows how. For these things, if it pleases, let us fashion letters that in no way cause a reader to be suspicious.*

<margin>Red</margin>
*By living justly we attain happiness: for only the humble are to be exalted, only the rash are to be condemned. If you seek happiness, hold to eternal justice, and you will attain the highest glory. Be a most fervent Zealot of truth and do not love a lie, which corrupts honesty and dissolves the temperance of the soul; flee the vanity of the world, for it is very brief. Be a lover of Justice and supplant no one unjustly. Cultivate virtues, flee vices, protect liberty with justice, beware of all insolence, and be always humble. Love the Savior of our souls: he who loves him shall by no means die unhappily. Despise glory that passes away suddenly; love the blessedness of the saints, which shall not be corrupted eternally.* <note>This entire paragraph of pious advice is a "cover text." The actual secret message is hidden within these words, likely extracted by taking the first letter of each word or following a specific numerical skip-key</note>

-># BOOK ONE.<-

->C 2<-

...[continuation from previous page] <note>original: "-dam", likely the end of a word like "quodam"</note>, carefully flee the snares of our ancient enemy <note>The Devil</note>, whom you will overcome by the never-ceasing humility of devotion. Be vigilant so that you are not conquered by the heat of the flesh; manfully defend the paths of justice, and remember that the zeal for righteousness must be held with fearless perseverance. Always flee vainglory as if it were poison; contemplate the ever-abiding pleasantness of the Paradise of God. The joys of the world pass quickly; today a man is joyful, tomorrow, stripped of everything, he dies. Flee the wicked zeal of bitterness, flee the swelling of pride, for you are not ignorant that the guilt of both is subject to perpetual evils. Every proud man is hateful to God: dying, he will be tormented without end and will never be comforted. What, finally, will the proud expect except lamentation? Therefore, woe to you, elated ones, worthy of eternal punishment and reserved for everlasting darkness and horrors. O vanity of the vanities of the world, destroyer of the light of souls, deceiver of exiles, most ruinous opener of hell.

->*Written by Johannes Trithemius, Abbot of Sponheim, on the 11th of March, 1500.* <note>original: "5. id. Martij M.D." — the 5th day before the Ides of March</note> ❧<-

When receiving the letter, once the sign of Barmiel is recognized, let the recipient immediately turn to the South as soon as he is able. For no danger can arise from delay regarding the obedience of the spirit, even if much time has passed. For when they call, he is always accustomed to come, whether quickly or slowly, even if they call after many days. This is his Conjuration, after those things which the art requires to be done have been performed:

**Barmiel any casleon archoi bulesan eris, Casray molaer pessaro duys anale goerno mesrue greal cusere drelnoz, parse cusureti basriel aslymaraphe neas lo, carnos erneo, damerosenotis anycarpodyn.** <note>This "conjuration" is a string of null-words and cipher-text; the actual secret message is hidden within these characters according to a pre-arranged system.</note> When he has said this Conjuration toward the South as is proper, he will hear and understand your mind, which you have committed not to letters but to the spirit; if he turns himself toward another quarter of the world, he will never understand.

### CHAPTER VI.

#### WHOSE SUPREME PRINCE IS GEDIEL

*> Dwelling in the South-Southwest <note>original: "Austro-africo"</note>, he has 20 ministers in the day, and as many in the night, whose office is to announce those things which involve dangers to be guarded against.*

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'S' depicting a landscape with figures and foliage.</image-desc>

**S**extending over the sixth mansion, which is in the South-Southwest, the great prince Gediel presides. He has under his dominion 20 other principal leaders during the day, and just as many at night, with many of their servants. The office of these is to report all things which pertain to advising a friend regarding any imminent...

First, before we proceed to the operation, you should know that at minimum two spirits are always to be called, along with their twenty servants, without whom they never go anywhere, even though they may not always appear visible. But this is not a cause for concern; you need only obtain through their invisible ministry what you intend. Therefore, when you wish to perform anything in <term>Steganography</term> <note>The "hidden writing" system Trithemius is teaching, which disguises messages as magic or prayers.</note> through their ministry; turn yourself toward the South-Southwest; <note>original: "Austro-africum"</note> because all of them dwell there with their prince, Gediel, at all times: and do what you know must be done according to the order. This is the Conjuration:

> Gediel asiel modebar mopiel, casmoyn, rochamurenu proys nasaron atido casmear yearsy maludym velachain demosar otiel masdurym sodiujel mestray seor amarlum, laueur pealo netus fabelron. <note>While this sounds like a magical incantation, it is actually a cipher-key where specific letters or positions correspond to the hidden message.</note>

Once these things are completed, the two named princes who were called will be present—good, benevolent, and safe: whom you ought not to fear: but commit to them confidently whatever you wish: and they will faithfully fulfill your command ☿ <note>The symbol for Mercury, the messenger of the gods, here representing the act of communication.</note> this sign shall be red.

*I have secretly learned of a plan to surrender the castle tonight through treachery and betrayal. I wish to advise my prince: I cannot do it by letters, since the enemies search everyone passing on the roads; I cannot do it through messengers, because I know my danger cannot be trusted to anyone at all. I will call a spirit, I commit the secret to it, and I send letters only as a formality—whatever I wish—so that the prince may know which spirit is to be called: and from the number of spirits (since otherwise you would not know a spirit was sent by me without letters) that one goes and announces the news: the prince then secures the castle.*

<margin>Red.</margin> <note>original: "Roth." This likely refers to the spirit Roth or the use of red ink for specific keywords in the cipher system.</note>

## LET THE FORM OF THE LETTERS BE THIS OR ANOTHER; WHICH, WHEN HE HAS READ IT, HE SHOULD FORBID GOING. <note>The author suggests this letter serves as a "cover" for the secret message; once the spirit delivers the real news, the physical letter becomes irrelevant or serves as a distraction.</note>

*The highest nobility of the human race and the highest virtue is to love God purely, to seek His glory, and to adore Him with righteousness of mind. For the humility of a pure heart will reign in Heaven, patience will exalt us, and only the soul of a man who is stripped of worldly things, fearing and loving God, will be established in true nobility. Love God, despise the world; flee the age of iniquities; love brotherly religion and the bond of peace, and the unity of those living rightly. Christ warns in the Gospel: We ought not to be anxious about superfluous human cares. Having (says the Holy Apostle) only the necessities, let us be content. Let us therefore have the love of God and the zeal for justice: because the truly solid riches are purity of soul, simplicity of heart, neglect of earthly honor, the cleansing of beauty, and the institution of holy customs. Let us be intent on good works, lest vices overwhelm us; let us live innocently in all righteousness as if we were to die tomorrow. Let us love God with a most pure heart, happily keeping His commandments; let us mourn for our sins with humility at all times, praying to the most pious Savior that He may remit our vices and pour into us His love and the humility of heart. Let us set aside the lusts of this world and vain and false honors, diligently reflecting on our death. To live virtuously, to love one's neighbor, to do injury to no one, to fear God, to give alms, to refresh the hungry, to defend the good, to rebuke the vices of the wicked, to persist in prayers, to despise the glory of the world, and to devote oneself to readings—these are the treasures of Christians. For the vain glory of the world is about to fall suddenly, as are the false riches which we shall leave behind unwillingly as we expire in a short time; justice alone is our happiness. Let our glorying be in our humility, and let the possession of riches remain with us at all times: the memory of death, the fear of judgment, and the remembrance of our sins.* ❧

## CHAPTER VII.

### **THE SUPREME PRINCE OF WHICH IS ASIRI-**
### *el, whose dwelling is toward the South-West, having under his dominion 20 leaders who*
### *preside over the day, and just as many who preside over the night: whose of-*
### *fice is to announce the counsels of prin-*
### *ces to their friends.*

The first and supreme prince of the seventh mansion is called Asiriel, and he lives in that region of the world which we call toward the South-West <note>original: "ad Africum," referring to the direction of the Africus wind.</note>. He has under him 20 princes who preside over the day, and just as many who preside over the night: all of whom have many <term>famuli</term> <note>servants or attendant spirits</note> under them. 

If two leaders from the day-spirits are called by the practitioner for the service of the art, they always have 20 servants serving them; and the same happens with the night-spirits. Therefore, if you call only one of the princes—especially from the night-spirits—it is established that he has only 10 servants, not 20. You shall not call the day-spirits except for at least two, since they are not accustomed to coming alone; from the night-spirits, you may call one or two, as you please. If you call two, you will know that 20 servants must be added in the conjuration according to custom; if one, 10. And so that you may know which ones are to be called alone and which with a companion, consider the table.

Of these spirits who are the principal ones among the forty, they have the duty of announcing the secret counsels of princes to their subjects and friends. You have eight from the day and just as many from the night, along with their many servants, who are sufficient for all things; there is no need to call the others. Observe diligently that when you call one or two princes from those already named, you must express the number of their servants in the song <note>Trithemius uses "carmen" (song/incantation) to refer to the rhythmic, coded messages used in his system.</note>. For they are proud, and it pleases them wonderfully that they are said to preside over many attendants. Therefore, when you wish to operate through them in <term>Steganography</term> <note>The art of hidden writing. In this context, it refers to the entire system of spirit-messengers.</note>, turn your face toward the South-West <note>original: "Africum," the direction of the wind blowing from North Africa toward Rome.</note>, and do what you know must be done with diligence, so that you omit absolutely nothing of those things which pertain to the art.

**Conjuration:** Asiriel aphorsy Lamodyn to Carmephyn drubal asutroy Sody barucho vsefer palormy thulnear asmerō chornemadulyn coleny busarethō duys marphelitubra nasaron venear fabelronty. <note>In the Steganographia, these "barbarous names" are actually a cipher. The first and last letters of words, or specific patterns within them, hide the actual message being sent.</note>

When these things are completed, the ones called will stand there. To distinguish them, know that the princes usually appear in an "airy" garment—that is, <gloss>Sapphire blue</gloss>—while the servants appear in white. Entrust your secret to the princes themselves, because they are faithful. All these leaders have a custom among themselves that when the practitioner addresses them, they immediately send the servants away; indeed, they withdraw on their own so that they do not hear the commission. When the practitioner stops speaking to the princes, the servants are seen to stand by again.

<margin>Roth: <note>Roth refers to a commentator or editor whose notes often appear in early editions.</note> Let the prince’s secret be such that he believes it should be delivered to no one else.</margin>

->The prince’s counsel is great and most secret, concerning difficult and momentous affairs.<-

The prince wishes another prince, some friend of his, to know this matter because he has a reason; but he fears to go out in public with the greatest danger to himself and his people. If, however, the matter were committed to a messenger, he fears betrayal; or if to a letter, he fears it might be read by someone. Therefore, he calls a spirit who will not reveal the secret. He entrusts it, sends it out, and it is perfected; he is secure.

<margin>Roth:</margin>
->He sends a letter that is not at all suspicious, fearing nothing of the public eye.<-

**J**ESUS Christ, unfailing Light, eternal happiness of all the saints. Creator and ruler of the universe, have mercy on us. We, the weak, groaning and weeping, ask for your mercy. Grant us the power to overcome the temptations of the devil, so that, having conquered present evils, we may obtain rest. O sweetest Savior Jesus, forgive the sins of us who pray. For we are your humble creatures, most clement Father: look upon us, your miserable and useless servants, because we desire to serve you with all our strength. Make us always possess the fervor of your love, adorned with good works. Give us the true workings of virtues, the observance of justice, and an inseparable love of righteousness: make us truly humble and vigorous despisers of worldly things, so that, always burning with your love, we may love nothing earthly and value nothing temporary as pleasant to us. O teacher of humility, Jesus Christ, teach us to imitate your doctrine. We are (alas!) entangled in many vices, naked of good works. Save us, Jesus Christ, lest we perish. O Highest Truth, give us life; most refreshing refuge of souls: be to us a tower of strength. For you are our life-giver, you are the salvation of souls, and the sweetest renewer of the mind. You are the unspeakable joy of angels and the most wholesome rest of faithful souls. You are the happiness of the saints who love you. You are the inextinguishable light of the heavenly citizens. You, the Giver of Life to the dead, look upon our fragility. Sweetest Jesus Christ, help us, your fragile servants, so that walking through the way of truth without offense, we may obtain eternal rest. O most pious Lord Jesus, mitigate the labors of our exile <note>In medieval theology, "exile" refers to the human condition on Earth, being away from the true "homeland" of Heaven.</note>. And when the end of this life begins to approach, deign then to help us mercifully, so that the adversary <note>The devil or spiritual enemy.</note> may fall terrified before the face of your power, and that the provokers of all evils, the devils, the princes of eternal death, and our most cruel persecutors may flee far away. We ask through the power of your most noble death, sweetest Jesus: do not despise us useless servants in the day of our departure, whom you willed to redeem with your blood. Wash away our excesses, so that we may deserve to enter the joys of the heavenly homeland, with the angels waiting for us. Amen. ☩

->I, Johannes Trithemius, Abbot, wrote this on the Ides of March, 1500.<- <note>original: "Scripsi ego Ioan. Trith. Abbas, idibus Martij, M.D." The Ides of March is March 15th.</note>
